# v0.13b ESCALATE labels (160 plans)

Copy labels_blank.csv to labels_a.csv (or labels_b.csv) and label `escalate` there by the escalate rule in cal/d11/LABELLING.md.

## T001

```text
1. Deploy DIV-034 dive team to inspect the vessel’s hull integrity, trapped water, and presence of hazardous materials or fuel, prioritizing safety before any lifting or movement begins.
2. Use DWP-060 submersible pump to remove trapped water from the vessel’s interior to improve stability and reduce weight before any lifting operations.
3. Deploy LB-038 lift bag set to assist in stabilizing the vessel and removing minor weight from the hull to reduce strain on the lifting point, while monitoring for any signs of further capsizing.
4. Position CRN-005 floating crane at Ashby Haven to begin the refloating operation, using its 390 t lift capacity to apply controlled force to the vessel’s neutral loading point or designated lifting point.
5. Deploy CRN-013 floating crane to provide additional lift capacity and support the refloating effort, combining its 350 t capacity with CRN-005 to reach 750 t total lift capacity.
6. Request immediate deployment of CRN-038 floating crane from its current location, despite ETA of 48.7 hours, to supplement lift capacity if the weather window permits and if the vessel’s condition allows for extended operations.
7. Coordinate with the naval architect to reassess the vessel’s stability and righting moment after water removal and initial lift, ensuring the refloating method is scaled appropriately for the vessel’s size and draft.
8. If the vessel remains capsized or unstable, initiate controlled deballasting or parbuckling using the combined lift capacity of CRN-005 and CRN-013, while monitoring for any risk of overturning.
9. If the vessel is fully inverted, implement upside-down refloating using the available lift capacity, ensuring lifting loads are controlled to avoid further capsizing.
10. If the vessel is on fire or shows signs of hazardous materials, immediately evacuate the area and activate fire suppression systems before any lifting begins, prioritizing crew safety.
11. If the vessel is sunken or requires deeper recovery, request additional assets or extend the weather window to allow for crane-and-barge removal or patching with a cofferdam, despite the 39.1-hour deadline.
12. If the vessel contains hazardous cargo or fuel, ensure all tanks are checked and offloaded before any cutting or movement begins, using the available resources to manage the risk.
13. If the vessel is aground, assess the seabed type and ground reaction to determine if lightering or tug assistance is needed, and monitor tide changes using a dedicated tide gauge if available.
14. If the vessel is too large or damaged for full refloat, request additional assets or consider section removal using crane-and-barge methods, despite the deadline.
15. If the vessel is a legacy or abandoned wreck, conduct a pollution risk assessment using standardized methods before deciding on recovery, prioritizing environmental safety.
16. If the vessel’s draft exceeds 10m, request tug assistance or additional lift capacity to ensure safe refloating, despite the deadline.
17. If the vessel’s size exceeds 50m, request additional assets or consider controlled deballasting to reduce weight before refloating, despite the deadline.
18. If the vessel’s draft is less than 3m, assess if natural refloat is possible on the next tide, and if not, use the available lift capacity to assist refloat.
19. If the vessel’s draft is between 3m and 10m, assess if tug assistance is needed for refloat, and if not, use the available lift capacity to assist refloat.
20. If the vessel’s draft is greater than 10m, request additional assets or consider controlled deballasting to reduce weight before refloating, despite the deadline.
21. If the vessel’s size is less than 10m, assess if manual or crane righting is sufficient, and if not, use the available lift capacity to assist refloating.
22. If the vessel’s size is between 10m and 50m, assess if controlled deballasting or parbuckling is sufficient, and if not, use the available lift capacity to assist refloating.
23. If the vessel’s size is greater than 50m, request additional assets or consider controlled deballasting to reduce weight before refloating, despite the deadline.
```

## T002

```text
1. Deploy TUG-022 and TUG-043 to the vessel’s position to begin applying bollard pull for refloating, with TUG-022 positioned on the starboard bow and TUG-043 on the port bow to provide balanced force.
2. Simultaneously, position WB-015 and WB-058 alongside the vessel’s midships to assist with stabilizing the hull and preventing excessive yaw during the refloat operation.
3. Monitor tide gauge readings near the vessel to track water level changes and adjust tug positioning as the tide rises to optimize ground reaction and minimize hull strain.
4. Confirm all fuel tanks are secured and checked for leaks before any movement begins, with TUG-022 and TUG-043 maintaining steady pull while WB-015 and WB-058 provide lateral support.
5. Apply full bollard pull from TUG-022, TUG-043, WB-015, and WB-058 to initiate refloat, with the combined force of 181 t exceeding the 150 t requirement to ensure successful refloat within 6.7 hours.
6. Once the vessel begins to rise, maintain steady pull and monitor hull reaction to avoid sudden movements that could cause structural damage or grounding on submerged obstructions.
7. After the vessel is fully refloated and clear of the shallows, secure it with mooring lines and prepare for towing to a safe anchorage or repair facility using TUG-022 and TUG-043 as primary towing assets.
```

## T005

```text
1. Deploy DIV-043 dive team to inspect the vessel’s hull integrity and check for hazardous materials or trapped water, prioritizing safety before any righting operations.
2. Activate WIN-012 shore pulling winch set to begin applying controlled righting moment, targeting the neutral loading point to minimize hull strain.
3. Activate WIN-017 shore pulling winch set to supplement WIN-012, combining their 800 t.m + 340 t.m = 1140 t.m righting moment.
4. Activate WIN-026 shore pulling winch set to add 800 t.m righting moment, bringing total on-scene moment to 1140 + 800 = 1940 t.m.
5. Activate WIN-028 shore pulling winch set to add 800 t.m righting moment, bringing total to 1940 + 800 = 2740 t.m.
6. Activate WIN-030 shore pulling winch set to add 360 t.m righting moment, bringing total to 2740 + 360 = 3100 t.m.
7. Activate WIN-044 shore pulling winch set to add 800 t.m righting moment, bringing total to 3100 + 800 = 3900 t.m.
8. Activate WIN-043 shore pulling winch set to add 800 t.m righting moment, bringing total to 3900 + 800 = 4700 t.m.
9. Activate WIN-015 shore pulling winch set to add 480 t.m righting moment, bringing total to 4700 + 480 = 5180 t.m.
10. Activate WIN-038 shore pulling winch set to add 800 t.m righting moment, bringing total to 5180 + 800 = 5980 t.m.
11. Activate WIN-053 shore pulling winch set to add 40 t.m righting moment, bringing total to 5980 + 40 = 6020 t.m.
12. Activate WIN-034 survey launch to monitor tide and ground reaction changes, ensuring refloat timing aligns with tidal cycles.
13. Activate WIN-014 shore pulling winch set (if available) to add further righting moment, but since not listed, proceed with current 6020 t.m.
14. Request immediate deployment of PBJ-008 parbuckling strand jack set from Port Haldane, ETA 64.3 h, to supplement righting moment beyond 4.8 h deadline.
15. Deploy BOOM-060 oil containment boom around the vessel to contain any potential fuel or hazardous material release during righting operations.
16. Coordinate with SVY-034 survey launch to provide real-time seabed and vessel position data to optimize winch load distribution.
17. Maintain continuous monitoring of hull stress and ground reaction using DIV-043 dive team and SVY-034 survey launch data.
18. If vessel remains capsized beyond 4.8 h, initiate controlled deballasting or parbuckling using PBJ-008 upon arrival, prioritizing stability checks before righting.
19. If hazardous materials are confirmed, halt all operations and deploy additional containment and decontamination assets before proceeding.
20. If fuel is detected, offload before any cutting or movement begins, using available winches to stabilize the vessel during fuel transfer.

Assumption: The vessel is capsized and requires righting moment. The current on-scene winch capacity (5580 t.m) is insufficient to meet the 11100 t.m requirement within 4.8 h. The plan relies on the assumption that the vessel’s neutral loading point is identified and weight removal is targeted appropriately. The PBJ-008 parbuckling strand jack set is the only asset capable of providing the additional 5520 t.m needed, but its ETA is 64.3 h, making it unavailable for the 4.8 h deadline. The plan prioritizes maximizing available resources within the time constraint and prepares for extended operations.
```

## T009

```text
1. Assess the vessel’s condition and grounding status using SVY-050 to map the seabed and hull contact points, and DIV-003 to inspect for trapped water and structural integrity before any lifting attempt.
2. Deploy LB-041 and LB-034 to begin controlled buoyancy adjustment by inflating lift bags at the vessel’s neutral loading point, targeting weight reduction to minimize strain on the hull and seabed.
3. Use BRG-035 to offload non-essential cargo and fuel from the vessel’s deck to reduce overall weight, prioritizing hazardous materials if present.
4. Position CRN-049 to provide primary lifting capacity, with LB-041 and LB-034 assisting to distribute load and prevent hull overturning during the refloat attempt.
5. Coordinate with DIV-003 to monitor for hazardous gas release during lifting operations and ensure all tanks are secured before any movement begins.
6. Execute controlled refloat using CRN-049 and LB-041, with LB-034 providing supplemental lift to maintain vessel stability as it clears the rocks.
7. Once clear of the rocks, use BRG-035 to secure the vessel and prepare for transport to a safe anchorage, with CRN-049 providing final stabilization.
8. Request additional lift capacity from CRN-001 and LB-025 to be deployed at 107.1 h, but note that the current 351 t capacity is insufficient to meet the 1400 t requirement within the 55.6 h window.
9. Assume the vessel is a medium-sized sail vessel with draft between 3m-10m, and that it contains non-hazardous cargo, based on visual evidence.
10. Limit the plan to the available assets and acknowledge that the required lift capacity cannot be met by the deadline, requesting a revised timeline or additional resources.
```

## T036

```text
1. Deploy dive team DIV-024 to assess the vessel’s condition, check for trapped water, hazardous materials, and structural integrity, and confirm if the vessel is aground, capsized, sunken, or on fire, using visual and sensor data from the site.

3. If the vessel is aground and requires refloating, deploy floating crane CRN-001 to begin lightering operations to reduce weight, targeting areas away from the neutral loading point to avoid strain.
4. If the vessel is capsized, deploy floating crane CRN-002 to initiate controlled deballasting or parbuckling, ensuring trapped water is cleared and stability criteria are met before righting.
5. If the vessel is sunken, deploy submersible pump DWP-059 to offload fuel and hazardous cargo before any hull disturbance, then deploy floating crane CRN-009 to begin patching and refloating in shallow water.
6. If the vessel is on fire, deploy fireboat monitors to cool adjacent spaces and suppress the fire using alcohol-resistant foam if water-soluble fuel is present, then deploy floating crane CRN-047 to begin recovery once the fire is extinguished.
7. Once the vessel is stabilized, deploy floating crane CRN-007 to provide additional lift capacity for refloating or recovery operations, ensuring the combined lift capacity remains within the 480 t requirement.
8. Monitor the tide and ground reaction using the dedicated tide gauge, adjusting the refloating or recovery method as the tide rises to optimize the pulling force and avoid overturning.
9. Deploy lift bag set LB-034 to assist in controlled lifting or buoyancy adjustment during refloating or recovery, ensuring loads are kept controlled to avoid further damage.
10. Conduct a final stability and structural assessment using the dive team DIV-024 and floating crane CRN-021 to ensure the vessel can be safely refloated or recovered without capsizing or sinking.
```

## T053

```text

8. Prepare for potential hull damage or structural failure by deploying a marine response crew to monitor for signs of hull breach or oil release; assume no dedicated response crew is available in the ledger.
9. If additional tugs are not available within the 4.0-hour window, initiate a request for a salvage contractor to deploy a crane or barge-based refloat system capable of providing the required 470 t bollard pull; assume no such contractor is available in the ledger.
10. If the vessel’s draft exceeds 10m or the seabed is rocky, request a dredging operation to clear the grounding area; assume no dredging equipment is available in the ledger.
11. If the vessel contains hazardous cargo, initiate a hazardous material assessment and containment protocol; assume no hazardous material assessment capability is available in the ledger.
12. If the vessel contains fuel, initiate a fuel offloading operation before any refloat attempt; assume no fuel offloading capability is available in the ledger.
13. If the vessel is found to be capsized or sunken, initiate a rescue and recovery protocol for trapped personnel and cargo; assume the vessel is not capsized or sunken based on visual evidence.
14. If the vessel is on fire, initiate a fire suppression protocol using foam or gas flooding; assume the vessel is not on fire based on visual evidence.
15. If the vessel’s hull is damaged or breached, initiate a patching or cofferdam operation to prevent further water ingress; assume no patching or cofferdam capability is available in the ledger.
16. If the vessel’s cargo is hazardous, initiate a cargo accounting and containment protocol; assume no cargo accounting capability is available in the ledger.
17. If the vessel’s hull is too damaged to refloat intact, initiate a section removal or crane-and-barge recovery protocol; assume no such capability is available in the ledger.
18. If the vessel’s grounding is hard or the seabed is coral or rock, initiate a dredging operation to clear the grounding area; assume no dredging equipment is available in the ledger.
19. If the vessel’s draft is less than 3m, initiate a refloat operation using the available 120 t bollard pull; assume the vessel’s draft exceeds 3m based on visual evidence.
20. If the vessel’s draft is between 3m and 10m, initiate a refloat operation using the available 120 t bollard pull; assume the vessel’s draft exceeds 10m based on visual evidence.
21. If the vessel’s draft exceeds 10m, initiate a refloat operation using the available 120 t bollard pull and request additional tugs; assume the vessel’s draft exceeds 10m based on visual evidence.
22. If the vessel’s grounding is soft or the seabed is mud or sand, initiate a refloat operation using the available 120 t bollard pull; assume the seabed is sand or mud based on visual evidence.
```

## T072

```text
1. Deploy LB-029 (Thorne Harbour, ETA 13.7 h) to begin initial lift setup at the casualty site, targeting the vessel’s neutral loading point to minimize hull strain during refloat.
2. Deploy LB-042 (Corran Harbour, ETA 4.9 h) to provide secondary lift capacity and redundancy, positioning it to assist LB-029 in stabilizing the vessel’s buoyancy during the refloat phase.
3. Deploy LB-053 (Thorne Harbour, ETA 12.6 h) to augment the lift capacity, ensuring the combined lift exceeds the 70 t requirement and allowing for controlled load distribution.
4. Deploy LB-047 (Thorne Harbour, ETA 14.4 h) to provide additional lift redundancy and support for the main lift operation, ensuring the vessel remains stable during the refloat sequence.
5. Deploy LB-051 (Thorne Harbour, ETA 14.6 h) to provide final lift capacity, ensuring the total combined lift capacity remains above 70 t and allowing for staged weight reduction if needed.
6. Deploy DIV-002 (Thorne Harbour, ETA 6.1 h) to conduct a preliminary hull integrity and hazardous material survey before any lifting or movement begins, ensuring no fuel or toxic cargo is present.
7. Deploy SVY-010 (underway, ETA 10.7 h) to conduct a seabed and ground reaction assessment, identifying the type of seabed and calculating the friction coefficient to determine the required pulling force for refloat.
8. Deploy BRG-059 (underway, ETA 10.3 h) to provide a floating platform for the lift operation, ensuring the vessel remains stable and level during the refloat process.
9. Execute controlled deballasting or parbuckling using LB-029, LB-042, LB-053, LB-047, and LB-051 to right and refloat the vessel, ensuring all lifts are synchronized to avoid overturning.
10. Monitor the tide using a dedicated tide gauge to track ground reaction changes and adjust lift timing to coincide with optimal tide conditions for refloat.
11. Once refloated, conduct a final stability check using the naval architect’s criteria to ensure the vessel is safe for further operations.
12. If the vessel contains any hazardous materials, conduct a full cargo accounting and fuel offloading before any further movement or cutting begins.
13. If the vessel is found to be aground, ensure all tanks are checked for fuel and hazardous cargo before any cutting or movement begins.
14. If the vessel is found to be capsized, prioritize crew rescue using the hand-over-hand technique before attempting to right the vessel.
15. If the vessel is found to be sunken, conduct sequential gas testing for explosive, oxygen, and toxic gases before any diver enters or hot work begins.
16. If the vessel is found to be on fire, confirm the affected space is clear of personnel before activating any fixed suppression system.
17. Once the vessel is refloated and stable, conduct a final hull and stability reassessment to ensure the vessel is safe for further operations.
18. If the vessel is a legacy or abandoned wreck, rank it for pollution risk using a standardized risk-ranking method before deciding whether to remove it.
19. If the vessel is found to be too deep or too damaged to raise intact, use crane-and-barge removal in sections, ensuring all cargo and hazardous materials are accounted for before disturbing the hull.
20. If the vessel is found to be releasing oil, contain it using methods matched to its behavior in water, and use side-scan or electro-acoustic sonar to locate submerged oil if necessary.
21. If the vessel is found to be in a sensitive habitat, prioritize diver-directed recovery over dredging, weighed through a net environmental benefit analysis.
22. If the vessel is found to be too large or too deep for the current lift capacity, request additional assets or adjust the refloat plan to accommodate the vessel’s size and draft.
23. If the vessel is found to be in a location requiring specialized equipment or expertise, request additional resources or adjust the refloat plan to accommodate the vessel’s location.
```

```text
4. Initiate a coordinated water delivery sequence: MON-054 (600 m3/h) and PMP-055 (300 m3/h) operate simultaneously to maintain a minimum 900 m3/h flow to the vessel’s aft section, while FB-022 (1000 m3/h) targets the midships fire zone, achieving a combined on-scene delivery of 1900 m3/h.
5. Request immediate deployment of DWP-057 submersible pump from Tolland Point, despite its ETA of 2.5 hours, to be prioritized for emergency water delivery if the current 1900 m3/h combined flow is insufficient to contain the fire before it reaches the fuel tanks, with the pump to be deployed at the vessel’s starboard side to avoid obstructing the fireboat’s access.
6. Assign SVY-030 survey launch to conduct a rapid hull integrity assessment and identify any ruptured fuel or hazardous cargo tanks, while maintaining a safe distance from the fire zone, to guide the deployment of additional resources and prevent further contamination.
7. Maintain continuous monitoring of the vessel’s stability and draft using tide gauge data from Maddock Pier, and prepare for potential hull collapse or capsizing by securing all available personnel and equipment to the vessel’s superstructure, while ensuring all fire suppression systems are activated and monitored.
8. If the fire is not contained within 4.8 hours, initiate a controlled deballasting operation using DWP-057 submersible pump to reduce the vessel’s draft and prevent sinking, while maintaining fire suppression efforts, with the pump to be deployed at the vessel’s port side to avoid obstructing the fireboat’s access.
9. If the vessel begins to capsize or sink, deploy all available resources to evacuate any remaining crew and initiate a controlled refloating operation using tug assistance and lightering, while maintaining fire suppression efforts to prevent further damage.
10. If the vessel is fully submerged, initiate a recovery operation using crane-and-barge removal in sections, while maintaining containment of any released oil using BOOM-008 and MON-054, and conduct a full environmental risk assessment using SVY-030 survey launch to determine the best course of action for the wreck.
```

```text
2. Deploy the dive team DIV-024 to conduct a preliminary underwater inspection of the vessel’s hull integrity, presence of trapped water, and any hazardous materials or fuel leaks, starting at T+1.9 h.
3. Deploy the submersible pump DWP-059 to begin removing trapped water from the vessel’s interior if present, to stabilize the hull and prepare for potential righting or refloating, starting at T+23.8 h.
4. Deploy the floating crane CRN-001 to the site at Dunmore Sound to begin setting up for lifting operations, starting at T+11.3 h.
5. Deploy the lift bag set LB-034 to assist in stabilizing or lifting small sections of the vessel if needed, starting at T+4.8 h.
6. Deploy the floating crane CRN-047 to the site at Marrow Inlet to provide additional lift capacity, starting at T+29.6 h.
7. Deploy the floating crane CRN-007 to the site at Port Ellery to provide additional lift capacity, starting at T+101.4 h (outside the 50.1 h window, so not available for the deadline).
8. Coordinate with the naval architect to confirm the vessel’s center of gravity and neutral loading point for any weight removal or shifting, using data from DIV-024’s inspection.
9. Begin controlled deballasting or parbuckling operations using CRN-001 and CRN-047 to right the vessel if it is capsized, or to refloat it if aground, starting at T+29.6 h.
10. If the vessel is sunken, initiate patching and cofferdam operations using DIV-024 and DWP-059 to prepare for crane-and-barge removal, starting at T+23.8 h.
11. If the vessel contains hazardous materials or fuel, initiate containment and offloading procedures using DWP-059 and DIV-024 before any lifting or cutting begins, starting at T+23.8 h.
12. Monitor the tide using a dedicated tide gauge (if available) to track ground reaction changes and adjust lifting operations accordingly, starting at T+11.3 h.
13. If the vessel is aground on a hard surface, initiate dredging operations using CRN-001 and CRN-047 to clear the channel, starting at T+29.6 h.
14. If the vessel is fully inverted, initiate upside-down refloating operations using CRN-001 and CRN-047, starting at T+29.6 h.
15. If the vessel is lying on its side, initiate side refloating operations using CRN-001 and CRN-047, starting at T+29.6 h.
16. If the vessel is on fire, initiate fire suppression using foam or gas flooding as appropriate, using DWP-059 and DIV-024, starting at T+23.8 h.
17. If the vessel is sunken and too deep or too damaged to raise intact, initiate crane-and-barge removal in sections using CRN-001 and CRN-047, starting at T+29.6 h.
18. If the vessel is capsized, prioritize crew rescue using DIV-024 before attempting to right the vessel, starting at T+1.9 h.
19. If the vessel is aground and requires lightering, initiate weight reduction operations using DWP-059 and DIV-024 to reduce strain on the hull, starting at T+23.8 h.
20. If the vessel is sunken and requires sequential testing for explosive gas, oxygen, and toxic gas, initiate testing using DIV-024 before any diver enters or hot work begins, starting at T+1.9 h.
21. If the vessel is sunken and requires oil containment, initiate containment using DWP-059 and DIV-024 to match the oil’s behavior in the water, starting at T+23.8 h.
22. If the vessel is sunken and requires diver-directed recovery, initiate recovery using DIV-024 and DWP-059 in sensitive habitat, starting at T+1.9 h.
23. If the vessel is sunken and requires legacy or abandoned wreck risk ranking, initiate risk ranking using DIV-024 and DWP-059, starting at T+1.9 h.
24. If the vessel is sunken and requires patching and refloat in shallow water, initiate patching and refloat using CRN-001 and CRN-047, starting at T+29.6 h.
25. If the vessel is sunken and requires patching with a cofferdam and pull in moderate depths, initiate patching and pull using CRN-001 and CRN-047, starting at T+29.6 h.
26. If the vessel is sunken and requires crane-and-barge removal in sections, initiate removal using CRN-001 and CRN-047, starting at T+29.6 h.
27. If the vessel is capsized and requires stability check against minimum righting-arm and metacentric-height criteria, initiate stability check using DIV-024 and CRN-001, starting at T+1.9 h.
28. If the vessel is capsized and requires trapped water clearance, initiate clearance using DWP-059 and DIV-024, starting at T+23.8 h.
29. If the vessel is capsized and requires righting method scaling to size, initiate righting using CRN-001 and CRN-047, starting at T+29.6 h.
30. If the vessel is capsized and requires lifting loads to be kept controlled, initiate controlled lifting using CRN-001 and CRN-047, starting at T+29.6 h.
31. If the vessel is aground and requires tug assistance to refloat, initiate tug assistance using CRN-001 and CRN-047, starting at T+29.6 h.
32. If the vessel is aground and requires dredging to clear a channel before refloat, initiate dredging using CRN-001 and CRN-047, starting at T+29.6 h.
33. If the vessel is aground and requires checking for fuel before any cutting or movement begins, initiate fuel checking using DIV-024 and DWP-059, starting at T+1.9 h.
34. If the vessel is aground and requires checking for hazardous cargo before any cutting or movement begins, initiate hazardous cargo checking using DIV-024 and DWP-059, starting at T+1.9 h.
35. If the vessel is aground and requires pulling force calculation based on ground reaction and friction coefficient, initiate calculation using DIV-024 and CRN-001, starting at T+1.9 h.
36. If the vessel is aground and requires neutral loading point targeting, initiate targeting using DIV-024 and CRN-001, starting at T+1.9 h.
37. If the vessel is sunken and requires sequential testing for explosive gas, then oxygen, then toxic gas, initiate testing using DIV-024, starting at T+1.9 h.
38. If the vessel is sunken and requires recovery method scaling to depth, initiate scaling using CRN-001 and CRN-047, starting at T+29.6 h.
39. If the vessel is sunken and requires fuel offloading before disturbing the hull, initiate offloading using DWP-059 and DIV-024, starting at T+23.8 h.
40. If the vessel is sunken and requires cargo accounting before disturbing the hull, initiate accounting using DIV-024 and DWP-059, starting at T+1.9 h.
41. If the vessel is sunken and requires released oil containment, initiate containment using DWP-059 and DIV-024, starting at T+23.8 h.
42. If the vessel is sunken and requires side-scan or electro-acoustic sonar to locate submerged oil, initiate sonar using DIV-024 and DWP-059, starting at T+1.9 h.
43. If the vessel is sunken and requires diver-directed recovery over dredging, initiate recovery using DIV-024 and DWP-059, starting at T+1.9 h.
44. If the vessel is sunken and requires standardized risk-ranking method for pollution risk, initiate ranking using DIV-024 and DWP-059, starting at T+1.9 h.
45. If the vessel is sunken and requires net environmental benefit analysis, initiate analysis using DIV-024 and DWP-059, starting at T+1.9 h.
46. If the vessel is sunken and requires patching
```

## T098

```text

3. Deploy SVY-019 to conduct a seabed survey to determine ground reaction and friction coefficient for refloat planning.
4. Deploy BOOM-029 around the vessel to contain any potential fuel or hazardous material leakage during refloat operations.
5. Establish a dedicated tide gauge near the vessel to monitor water level changes and predict optimal refloat window.
6. Coordinate with naval architect to identify neutral loading point and target weight reduction areas if lightering is required.
7. Initiate refloat operation using all available tugs once the tide reaches optimal level and ground reaction is favorable.
8. Monitor hull integrity and stability during refloat; if hull shows signs of structural failure, halt operation and request structural assessment.
9. If refloat fails, initiate controlled deballasting or parbuckling using available assets, prioritizing crane or barge support if required.
10. If vessel remains aground after refloat attempts, request dredging support to clear channel and reattempt refloat.
```

## T101

```text
1. Assess the vessel’s grounding condition and confirm it is aground, not capsized or sunken, based on its silhouette and visible structure; request a dedicated tide gauge deployment near the site to monitor ground reaction changes.
2. Commit AHT-022 and AHT-032 to the refloat operation, combining their 220 t and 200 t bollard pull for a total of 420 t, which is insufficient to meet the 950 t requirement.
3. Request immediate deployment of additional anchor handling tugs with bollard pull capacity exceeding 530 t to supplement the current 420 t, as no available assets in the ledger can meet the shortfall.
4. Request a second tide gauge to be deployed at a second location near the vessel to provide redundant data on seabed reaction and tide rise rate.
5. Request a marine surveyor to assess the seabed type (mud, sand, coral, or rock) to determine the friction coefficient for calculating the required pulling force and to identify potential dredging needs if the grounding is hard.
6. Request a neutral loading point analysis to be performed on the vessel’s hull to determine where weight reduction (if any) would be most effective without increasing strain on the grounding points.
7. Request a lightering plan to be developed if the vessel’s draft exceeds 10m, including identification of cargo or fuel that can be offloaded to reduce weight and improve refloatability.
8. Request a tug assistance plan to be developed if the vessel’s draft exceeds 10m, including identification of tugs capable of providing additional pulling force beyond the current 420 t.
9. Request a refloat simulation to be performed using the available 420 t and projected additional tug capacity to determine the optimal refloat strategy and timing relative to the tide.
10. Request a refloat timeline to be developed that includes the estimated time for the tide to rise sufficiently to reduce ground reaction, and the time required for the full 950 t of pulling force to be available.
11. Request a refloat safety plan to be developed that includes procedures for monitoring the vessel’s stability during refloat, and for managing any potential hull damage or structural failure.
12. Request a refloat contingency plan to be developed that includes procedures for managing any unexpected delays or failures in the refloat operation, including the potential need for additional resources or alternative refloat methods.
13. Request a refloat environmental plan to be developed that includes procedures for managing any potential oil or hazardous material release during the refloat operation, including the deployment of BOOM-037 if necessary.
14. Request a refloat communication plan to be developed that includes procedures for coordinating the refloat operation with all involved assets and personnel, and for reporting progress and any issues to the command center.
15. Request a refloat documentation plan to be developed that includes procedures for documenting all refloat activities, including the time, resources used, and any issues encountered, for future reference and analysis.
16. Request a refloat debrief plan to be developed that includes procedures for conducting a post-refloat review to identify any lessons learned and to improve future refloat operations.
17. Request a refloat resource allocation plan to be developed that includes procedures for allocating resources to the refloat operation based on their availability and capability, and for managing any resource conflicts or shortages.
18. Request a refloat risk assessment plan to be developed that includes procedures for identifying and managing any risks associated with the refloat operation, including the potential for hull damage, environmental impact, or personnel injury.
19. Request a refloat training plan to be developed that includes procedures for training all involved personnel on the refloat operation, including their roles and responsibilities, and any safety procedures.
20. Request a refloat equipment check plan to be developed that includes procedures for checking all equipment used in the refloat operation, including tugs, booms, and other assets, to ensure they are in good working condition and ready for use.
```

```text

3. Deploy WIN-037 shore pulling winch set to apply controlled tension along the vessel’s port side, using its 800 t.m righting moment to complement SPN-025 and begin generating a righting couple.
4. Deploy WIN-029 shore pulling winch set to apply controlled tension along the vessel’s starboard side, using its 340 t.m righting moment to further balance the righting moment distribution and prevent hull strain.
5. Deploy BOOM-031 oil containment boom around the vessel’s perimeter to contain any potential fuel or cargo leakage during the righting operation, minimizing environmental impact.

7. Deploy RCB-041 crane barge, rated righting moment 56600 t.m, to position itself alongside the vessel’s starboard side and begin applying controlled lifting loads to the vessel’s upper hull to initiate parbuckling.
8. Deploy PBJ-012 parbuckling strand jack set to apply controlled righting force at the vessel’s port side, using its 3200 t.m righting moment to assist RCB-041 in generating the necessary righting couple.
9. Deploy RCB-008 crane barge, rated righting moment 36000 t.m, to position itself alongside the vessel’s port side and apply controlled lifting loads to the vessel’s upper hull to assist in the parbuckling process.
10. Coordinate all winch sets (WIN-015, WIN-021, WIN-029, WIN-037) to apply synchronized tension to the vessel’s hull, ensuring the righting moment is distributed evenly and preventing hull overturning during the righting process.
11. Monitor the vessel’s progress using a dedicated tide gauge to track ground reaction changes as the tide rises and adjust winch and jack set loads accordingly.
12. Once the vessel is upright or near upright, deploy RCB-008 and RCB-041 to apply controlled lifting loads to the vessel’s upper hull to ensure it remains stable and does not list again.
13. Deploy DIV-026 dive team to inspect the vessel’s internal compartments for any remaining trapped water or hazardous materials before any further movement or refloating.
14. Deploy WIN-015 shore pulling winch set to apply controlled tension along the vessel’s starboard side, using its 40 t.m righting moment to assist in stabilizing the vessel during the final stages of righting.
15. Deploy WIN-029 shore pulling winch set to apply controlled tension along the vessel’s port side, using its 340 t.m righting moment to assist in stabilizing the vessel during the final stages of righting.
16. Deploy WIN-037 shore pulling winch set to apply controlled tension along the vessel’s starboard side, using its 800 t.m righting moment to assist in stabilizing the vessel during the final stages of righting.
17. Deploy SPN-025 sponson / buoyancy caisson set to provide additional buoyancy to the vessel’s port side to ensure it remains stable during the final stages of righting.
18. Deploy WIN-021 shore pulling winch set to apply controlled tension along the vessel’s starboard side, using its 40 t.m righting moment to assist in stabilizing the vessel during the final stages of righting.
19. Deploy BOOM-031 oil containment boom to contain any potential fuel or cargo leakage during the final stages of righting, minimizing environmental impact.
20. Once the vessel is upright and stable, deploy RCB-008 and RCB-041 to apply controlled lifting loads to the vessel’s upper hull to ensure it remains stable and does not list again.
```

## T107

```text
1. Assess the vessel’s condition and confirm it is aground, not capsized or sunken, based on visible hull orientation and waterline; commit to refloating via lightering and tug assistance.
```

```text
11. If the vessel is aground, assess seabed conditions via SVY-010 and adjust lift strategy to avoid further grounding or hull damage.
12. If the vessel is on fire, immediately evacuate all personnel and activate fire suppression systems before initiating any lift operations.
13. If hazardous materials are detected, halt all operations and request additional resources for containment and decontamination before proceeding.
14. If the vessel is found to be too heavy or structurally compromised for the available lift capacity, request additional assets or extend the weather window beyond 16.9 h.
15. If the vessel is found to be in a position where refloating is not feasible, initiate salvage operations to remove cargo or hazardous materials before attempting to refloat or right the vessel.
```


## T127

```text
1. Assess the vessel "FORT MERCER" as capsized based on visual evidence of its inverted position in the water, and prioritize crew rescue before any structural intervention; deploy DIV-001 dive team to inspect for trapped personnel and clear any water ingress from the hull.
2. Confirm the vessel’s size and draft: as a large (>50m) merchant/commercial vessel with deep draft (>10m), it requires heavy-lift equipment for any righting or refloating; request immediate deployment of HLB-034 heavy lift crane barge (5000 t lift capacity) and HLB-059 heavy lift crane barge (4300 t lift capacity) to arrive within the 30.2-hour window.
3. Due to the capsized condition, initiate a controlled righting operation using HLB-034 and HLB-059; employ parbuckling or controlled deballasting methods scaled for large vessel size; commit both barges to the task for the duration of the operation.
4. Before any movement, check all tanks for fuel and hazardous cargo; deploy DWP-002 submersible pump to remove any residual fuel or hazardous liquids from the hull to reduce risk during righting.
5. Monitor the tide and ground reaction using a dedicated tide gauge; schedule righting operations to coincide with high tide to minimize friction and ground reaction forces; adjust the righting method based on seabed type if known.
6. Once the vessel is righted, assess hull integrity and stability; if the hull is intact, proceed with refloating using the combined lift capacity of HLB-034 and HLB-059; if the hull is compromised, consider section removal via crane-and-barge method.
7. Offload any cargo and hazardous materials before disturbing the hull; use CRN-056 floating crane to assist with small-scale cargo removal if needed, though its 100 t capacity is insufficient for the main task.
8. If the vessel contains oil or other pollutants, deploy containment booms and monitor for surface or submerged oil using side-scan sonar; request environmental specialists to assess pollution risk and implement mitigation measures.
9. After refloating, conduct a full stability check against minimum righting-arm and metacentric-height criteria; if the vessel is unstable, consider ballasting or additional weight distribution to ensure safe navigation.
10. Request additional assets to meet the 27100 t lift requirement; since the current fleet only provides 7000 t on scene within 30.2 hours, the operation cannot be completed with available resources; submit a request for additional heavy-lift assets with lift capacity exceeding 20100 t to be deployed within the weather window.
```

## T128

```text
1. Deploy TUG-022 and TUG-043 to the vessel’s location to provide initial bollard pull support, with TUG-022 arriving at T+4.7h and TUG-043 at T+6.2h.
2. Deploy WB-015 and WB-058 to assist with auxiliary pulling and positioning, with WB-015 arriving at T+1.4h and WB-058 at T+1.0h.
3. Coordinate all available tugs and workboats to apply maximum combined bollard pull of 80 t by T+6.7h, focusing pull force on the vessel’s midships to avoid strain on the bow or stern.
4. Monitor tide gauge readings near the vessel to track water level changes and adjust pulling strategy to maximize refloat efficiency as the tide rises.
5. Conduct a pre-refloat stability and ground reaction assessment using visual inspection and seabed mapping to determine if the vessel is aground on sand or mud and if lightering is needed.
6. If the vessel is confirmed aground on sand, initiate controlled refloat by maintaining continuous bollard pull from TUG-022, TUG-043, WB-015, and WB-058 until the vessel clears the seabed.
7. If the vessel is confirmed aground on mud, increase pulling force incrementally to avoid hull damage and maintain vessel alignment with the tide’s direction.
8. If the vessel is confirmed capsized or sunken, immediately request additional assets and initiate a separate salvage plan for righting or recovery, as the current fleet cannot meet the 150 t bollard pull requirement.
9. If hazardous materials or fuel are suspected, initiate containment and offloading procedures before any movement begins, using available resources to secure the area.
10. If the vessel is confirmed sunken, initiate a separate recovery plan using patching, cofferdam, or crane-and-barge methods, as the current fleet cannot meet the 150 t bollard pull requirement.
11. If the vessel is confirmed on fire, immediately request additional assets and initiate a separate firefighting plan, as the current fleet cannot meet the 150 t bollard pull requirement.
12. If the vessel is confirmed aground and the tide is insufficient to refloat naturally, continue pulling with the available assets until the vessel is free or until additional assets arrive.
13. If the vessel is confirmed capsized, initiate a separate righting plan using crane or parbuckling methods, as the current fleet cannot meet the 150 t bollard pull requirement.
```

## T132

```text
1. Deploy TUG-013 and TUG-029 to the vessel’s location to provide combined bollard pull of 102 t, with TUG-013 arriving at T+2.3 h and TUG-029 at T+2.9 h, to begin refloating efforts as soon as possible.
2. Position TUG-013 and TUG-029 to apply pulling force at the vessel’s neutral loading point, if identifiable, or at the bow and stern to minimize hull strain during refloat.
3. Monitor tide using a dedicated tide gauge to track ground reaction changes and adjust pulling force as the tide rises to optimize refloat timing.
4. Conduct a stability and structural assessment of the vessel using DIV-005 to check for trapped water and hull integrity before applying significant pulling force.
5. If the vessel is found to be aground on rock or coral, initiate dredging operations with BG-051 to clear the seabed and reduce ground reaction force.
6. If the vessel is found to be aground on sand or mud, continue pulling with TUG-013 and TUG-029 while monitoring for hull deformation and adjusting force to avoid structural failure.
7. If the vessel is found to be capsized or sunken, initiate a full hazard assessment with DIV-005 and SVY-027 to determine if refloat is feasible or if recovery requires patching or section removal.
8. If the vessel is found to be on fire, activate fire suppression systems with SVY-027 and DIV-005 to extinguish the fire before any refloat or recovery operations.
9. If hazardous materials are detected, initiate containment and offloading procedures with SVY-027 and DIV-005 before any further movement or refloat.
10. If the vessel is found to be fully inverted, initiate upside-down refloating procedures with TUG-013 and TUG-029, using controlled deballasting if necessary.
11. If the vessel is found to be lying on its side, initiate side refloating procedures with TUG-013 and TUG-029, using parbuckling if necessary.
12. If the vessel is found to be sunken, initiate patch and refloat procedures with BG-051 and TUG-013, or crane-and-barge removal if the hull is too damaged to raise intact.
13. If the vessel is found to be aground and requires lightering, initiate lightering operations with WB-010 and WB-038 to reduce weight before refloat.
14. If the vessel is found to be aground and requires tug assistance, initiate tug assistance with TUG-013 and TUG-029 to refloat the vessel.
15. If the vessel is found to be aground and requires dredging, initiate dredging operations with BG-051 to clear the seabed and reduce ground reaction force.
16. If the vessel is found to be aground and requires a neutral loading point, initiate weight shifting operations with WB-010 and WB-038 to target areas other than the neutral loading point to reduce strain.
17. If the vessel is found to be aground and requires a dedicated tide gauge, initiate tide gauge deployment with SVY-027 to track ground reaction changes.
18. If the vessel is found to be aground and requires a stability check, initiate stability check with DIV-005 to determine if refloat is feasible.
19. If the vessel is found to be aground and requires a hull assessment, initiate hull assessment with DIV-005 to determine if refloat is feasible.
20. If the vessel is found to be aground and requires a cargo assessment, initiate cargo assessment with SVY-027 to determine if refloat is feasible.
21. If the vessel is found to be aground and requires a hazardous material assessment, initiate hazardous material assessment with DIV-005 to determine if refloat is feasible.
22. If the vessel is found to be aground and requires a fuel assessment, initiate fuel assessment with SVY-027 to determine if refloat is feasible.
23. If the vessel is found to be aground and requires a refloat assessment, initiate refloat assessment with TUG-013 and TUG-029 to determine if refloat is feasible.
24. If the vessel is found to be aground and requires a refloat plan, initiate refloat plan with TUG-013 and TUG-029 to determine if refloat is feasible.
```

```text
4. Deploy PBJ-029 parbuckling strand jack set, 23700 t.m righting moment, Port Calder 6 nm, ETA 20.9 h, to assist in controlled righting and stabilize the vessel’s rotation.
5. Deploy SPN-025 sponson / buoyancy caisson set, 1600 t.m righting moment, Port Calder 6 nm, ETA 11.3 h, to provide additional buoyancy and counterbalance during the righting process.
6. Deploy WIN-021 shore pulling winch set, 40 t.m righting moment, Port Calder 6 nm, ETA 2.9 h, to apply controlled lateral tension and assist in aligning the vessel during righting.
7. Deploy WIN-026 shore pulling winch set, 800 t.m righting moment, Elden Bay 357 nm, ETA 30.5 h, to provide additional pulling force for hull alignment once the vessel begins to right.
8. Deploy WIN-029 shore pulling winch set, 340 t.m righting moment, Elden Bay 357 nm, ETA 27.4 h, to assist in fine-tuning the vessel’s position during the righting sequence.
9. Deploy WIN-037 shore pulling winch set, 800 t.m righting moment, Elden Bay 357 nm, ETA 26.8 h, to provide additional pulling force for hull alignment and stabilization.
10. Deploy WIN-056 shore pulling winch set, 800 t.m righting moment, Elden Bay 357 nm, ETA 29.0 h, to provide additional pulling force for hull alignment and stabilization.
11. Deploy BOOM-031 oil containment boom, 620 m, Port Calder 6 nm, ETA 2.7 h, to contain any potential oil leakage during the righting process.
12. Deploy DIV-026 dive team, 4 divers, Tolland Point 71 nm, ETA 4.7 h, to inspect the hull integrity and check for trapped water or hazardous materials before and during righting.
13. Monitor tide gauge readings near the site to track ground reaction changes as the tide rises and falls, adjusting righting force application accordingly.
14. Conduct stability check against minimum righting-arm and metacentric-height criteria before initiating righting to ensure safe vessel recovery.
15. Clear trapped water from the hull before commencing righting to prevent further instability.

18. Once the vessel is upright, assess hull integrity and check for fuel or hazardous cargo before any further movement or offloading.
19. If necessary, offload fuel and cargo before any cutting or movement begins to reduce weight and prevent further damage.
20. After righting, secure the vessel and prepare for refloating or further recovery operations as required.
```

## T134

```text
1. Deploy MON-014, MON-015, MON-059, MON-060, PMP-021, PMP-026, PMP-030, and PMP-039 to deliver water to the burning vessel, prioritizing assets with ETA ≤ 1.5 hours to ensure full capability is on scene within 1.9 hours.
2. Activate the fixed suppression system on the burning vessel once the affected space is confirmed clear of personnel, using water delivered by the committed assets.
3. Monitor the fire’s progression and hull integrity continuously, reassessing stability and risk of sinking or capsizing as the fire is suppressed.
4. Once the fire is extinguished, deploy SVY-049 to conduct a preliminary survey of the vessel’s structural integrity and cargo hold conditions to determine next steps for salvage or environmental response.
5. If hazardous materials or fuel are confirmed, initiate offloading procedures using available resources before any cutting or movement begins, prioritizing containment of any released oil.
6. If the vessel is aground or capsized, deploy tug assistance and/or lightering resources as needed, based on draft and grounding severity, to prepare for refloating or righting.
7. If the vessel is sunken, deploy divers to conduct sequential gas testing (explosive, oxygen, toxic) before any hot work or recovery begins, using resources available at Holm Harbour.
8. If the vessel is on fire and the fire reaches fuel tanks, maintain water delivery at 650 m3/h for the full 1.9 hours using the committed assets, with no reduction in flow until the fire is fully extinguished.
9. After fire suppression, assess the vessel’s draft and stability to determine if refloating or recovery operations can proceed safely, using the naval architect’s assessment as the baseline.
10. If refloating is required, deploy dredging or tug assistance as needed, based on seabed type and ground reaction, using resources available at Holm Harbour.
11. If the vessel is a legacy or abandoned wreck, initiate a pollution risk ranking using standardized methods before deciding on removal or in situ management.
12. Coordinate with environmental response teams to contain any oil spill, using side-scan sonar if submerged oil is suspected, and deploy containment booms as needed.
13. Once the vessel is stabilized, begin recovery operations using crane-and-barge removal if the hull is too damaged to raise intact, or patch-and-refloat if in shallow water.
14. Account for all cargo and hazardous materials before disturbing the hull, using the survey launch and available personnel to document and secure remaining cargo.
15. After recovery, conduct a final inspection of the vessel’s structure and environmental impact, and report findings to the incident command for post-incident analysis.
```

## T136

```text
1. Assess the vessel’s condition and confirm it is capsized, not aground or sunken, based on visual evidence of its inverted position and visible deck structures above water; commit to a capsized recovery plan.
2. Deploy the dive team DIV-004 to inspect the capsized vessel’s interior for trapped water, structural integrity, and presence of hazardous materials or fuel; prioritize crew safety and avoid hot work until clearance is confirmed.
3. Use the survey launch SVY-056 to map the vessel’s exact position, orientation, and seabed contact points to determine the optimal righting method and calculate the required lifting loads.
4. Deploy the heavy lift crane barge HLB-011 to the scene at Dunmore Sound; its 4400 t lift capacity will be used to initiate controlled deballasting or parbuckling operations to begin righting the vessel.
5. Coordinate with the dive team DIV-004 to monitor the vessel’s stability during righting and confirm the neutral loading point to avoid excessive strain on the hull during weight shifting.
6. Begin controlled deballasting using the submersible pump DWP-055 to reduce the vessel’s weight and shift its center of gravity; pump operations will be monitored by SVY-056 to ensure safe water removal without destabilizing the vessel.
7. Once deballasting is complete and the vessel’s stability is confirmed, use the heavy lift crane barge HLB-011 to apply controlled lifting loads to the vessel’s side to initiate the righting process.
8. Monitor the vessel’s progress and adjust lifting loads as needed to avoid overturning; continue until the vessel is upright or sufficiently stable for refloating.
9. After righting, use the heavy lift crane barge HLB-011 to refloat the vessel by applying controlled lifting forces to its hull, ensuring the vessel’s draft remains within safe limits.
10. Once refloated, conduct a final inspection using the dive team DIV-004 to confirm the vessel’s structural integrity and ensure no hazardous materials are leaking before any further operations are initiated.
11. Request additional heavy lift capacity beyond the available 4400 t to meet the 17500 t requirement; note that the current fleet cannot meet the deadline and recommend a revised plan or extended weather window.
```

```text
28. If the vessel is sunken, offload fuel before disturbing the hull.
29. If the vessel is sunken, account for cargo before disturbing the hull.
30. If the vessel is sunken, contain any released oil using methods matched to its behavior in water.
```

```text
2. Request immediate deployment of all available anchor handling tugs (AHT-007, AHT-046) and harbor tug (TUG-037) to arrive on scene within 6.9 hours, despite their combined 354 t bollard pull falling short of 880 t requirement.
3. Deploy workboats (WB-002, WB-010) to assist with positioning and minor winch operations, adding 8 t bollard pull to the total, bringing combined on-scene pull to 362 t by T+47.0 h.
4. Request additional tug resources from nearby ports or fleet command to supplement the 362 t available, as the current fleet cannot meet the 880 t requirement within the 6.9-hour window.
5. Deploy dive team (DIV-045) to inspect hull integrity and check for hazardous materials or fuel before any refloating attempt, as required for safety and environmental protection.
6. Deploy submersible pump (DWP-003) to offload any residual fuel or water from the hull, reducing weight and potential hazards before refloating.
7. Coordinate with tide gauge operators to monitor tidal changes and determine optimal refloating window, as the vessel may require a higher tide to refloat.
8. Prepare for lightering operations if necessary, by identifying and removing non-essential cargo or ballast to reduce weight and improve refloating chances.
9. Use crane or parbuckling method for refloating, as the vessel is large (>50m) and aground on a beach, requiring controlled lifting or deballasting.
10. Request additional assets or extended time to meet the 880 t bollard pull requirement, as the current fleet cannot refloat the vessel within the 6.9-hour deadline.
```

## T142

```text
5. Apply controlled pulling force to the vessel’s bow and stern using the tugs, maintaining a friction coefficient appropriate for the seabed type (assumed to be sand or mud based on visual context).
6. Once the vessel begins to rise, adjust tug forces to maintain neutral loading point alignment and prevent strain on the hull.
7. After the vessel is fully refloated, conduct a stability check and assess for any residual grounding or hull damage before proceeding to the next phase.
8. If hazardous materials are suspected, deploy BOOM-037 to contain any potential oil release during the refloat operation.
9. Once refloated, secure the vessel to a barge or dock using TUG-045 and TUG-050 for stabilization and to prevent further movement.
10. Request a full cargo and hazardous material inventory from the vessel’s owner or salvor to ensure safe handling and disposal of any remaining cargo.
```

```text
6. Coordinate with the survey launch SVY-007 to monitor the tide and ground reaction changes; use a dedicated tide gauge to track refloat timing as the tide rises.

8. Once the vessel is righted, use the sheerlegs SHL-045 and floating crane CRN-041 to secure and refloat the vessel; ensure all tanks are checked for fuel and hazardous cargo before any movement begins.
9. If the vessel is refloated, use the deck cargo barge BRG-026 to remove any remaining cargo or fuel; if the vessel is too damaged to refloat intact, plan for crane-and-barge removal in sections.
10. Conduct a final inspection using the dive team DIV-053 to ensure no hazardous materials are present and that the vessel is stable before releasing it for further assessment.
```
